Content note: Bishop Challoner's prayer book for English Catholic lay people, first issued in 1740: the summary of doctrine, morning and evening prayers, devotions for Mass, confession and communion; the book that shaped English Catholic piety for a century.

Inſtruftions for Meditations. 3g 


In order to the knowledge of ourſelves, our 


paſſions and vices, Cc. 
The method of meditation preſcribed by 


that great maſter in ſpirituality, St. Francis de 


Sales, in his introduction, part the 2d, is as 


follows: Fin, Place yourſelf in the preſence 
of God, by a lively faith that he ray and be- 


holds you, and is moſt intimately preſent in 
the very centre of your ſoul; proſtrate your- 
ſelf in ſpirit before him, to adore this ſove- 
reign Lord, whofe majeſty nlls heaven and 
earth: make an offering of your whole being 
to him ; and humbly beg his pardon for all 
your paſt treaſons and ſins. 

Secondly, Implore with fervour and humili- 
ty his light and grace, that you may perform 
this important exerciſe as you ought. 

Thirdly, Conſider attentively upon the ſub- 
ject which you have choſen for your medita- 
tion (which you ought to have prepared over 
night) and let the truths of heaven fink deep 
into your ſoul. Dwell moſt upon ſuch points 
as you find yourfe!f moſt affected with. 

Fourthly, From theſe conſiderations draw 
pious a Fections of the love of God, of grati- 
tude for his benefits, repentance for your fins, 
and the like, which are the principal part of 
mental prayer, and what you ought moſt to 
inſiſt upon. 

Fi th, From theſe affetions paſs on to 
refolutions of a ferious amendment of 


your life, particularly with regard to ſuch 

failings as you are moſt ſubject to: and de- 

termine with pans; to begin that very Gay 
5 


to put theſe good purpoſes in execution on 
ſuch occaſions as ſhall offer. 

Sixthly, Conclude by thankſgiving to God 
for the affections and reſolutions he has given 
you; offer them to him, and beg his bleſſing 

on the. . 

Seventhly, Lay up in your mind ſuch points 
of your meditation as have touched you moſt, 
and oftentimes in the day reflect upon them. 
Which the ſaint compares to the gathering, 


as it were, a noſegay, in this garden of devo- 
tion to ſmell at all the day. 


E::bthly, Such as find difficulty in medita- 
tion may help themſelves, by uſing ſome good 
book, reading leiſurely and pauſing upon 
what they read, and drawing — affections 
and reſolutions from it.

The Beginning of the Goſpel of St. John. 
ÞY the- beginning was the word, and the 
word was with God, and the word was 
God. The fame was in the beginning with 
God. All things were made by him, and 
without him was made nothing that was 
made. In him was life, and the life was the 
light of men; and the light ſhineth in dark- 
neſs, and the darkneſs has not comprehend- 
ed it. There was a man ſent from God, 
whoſe name was Fohn. This man came for 
a witneſs to give teſtimony of the light, that 
all men might believe through him. He was 
not the light, but to give teſtimony of the 
loke. That was the true light, which en- 
lightens every man that cometh into this 
world. He was in the world, and the world 
was made by him, and the world knew him 
not. He came into his own, and his own 
received him not. But as many as received 
him, he gave them power to be made the. 
ſons of God; to them that believe in his 
name, who are born not of blood, nor of the 
will of fleſh, nor of the will of man, but of 
God. And the word was made fleſh, and. 
dwelt among us. And we ſaw his glory, 
the glory as of the only-begotten. of the Fa- 
ther full of grace and truth.

the amice, which is firſt put upon the head, 
repreſents divine hope, which the apoſtle calls 
the helmet of ſalvation; the alb, innocence of 
life ; the girdle (with which the loins are be-

chaſuble, which is uppermoſt, and covers all 
the reſt, repreſents the virtue of Charity. 

| In theſe veſtments the church makes uſe 
of five colours, viz. the white on the feaſts 
of our Lord, of the B. Virgin, of the angels, 


and of the ſaints that were not martyrs; the 


red on the feaſts of Pentecoſt, of the inven- 
tion and exaltation of the croſs, and of the 
apoſtles and martvrs ; the violet, which is the 
penitential colour, in the penitential times 
of Advent and Lent, and upon Fisuls and 
Ember- Days ; the green on moſt of the other 
Sundays and Ferias throughout the year; and 
the black on Goad- Friday, and in the maſſes 
for the dead. | |

our aitar too, inaſmuch as we offer our 
prayers and facrifices through him. Upon

have betore their eyes during this ſacrifice,

if they have a family, fummoning them to- 
gether) let them go into their oratory, and

altar of God: after having bewailed their 
* misfortune in being kept at a diſtance from

ſelves in heart and affeftion with all that are 


offering this ſacrifice to God at that time;

commonly ule in time of hearing maſs.

Now the catholic faith is this: that we 


_ worſhip one God in Trinity, and Trinity in 
_ Vaity. 


Neither confounding the Perſons, nor di- 
viding the Subſtance. 

For one is the Perſon of the Father, an- 
other of the Son, another of the Holy Ghoſt. 

But the Godhead of the Father, and of 


the glory _ the majeſty co-eternal. 
Such as the Father is, ſuch is the Son, and 
ſuch is the Holy Ghoſt. 
The F ater: is uncreated, the Son is un» 
created, and the Holy Ghoſt i is uncreated. 
The Father is 6 the Son 
is incomprehenſible, and the Holy Ghoſt is 
incompretenſible. 
The Father is eternal, the Son is eternal, 


and the Holy a 
And yet they are not three Eternals, but

hearty eee one Unereated.

In like manner the Father is Almighty, 
the Son is Almighty, and the Holy Ghoſt is 
Almighty. 

And yet they are not three Almightics, but 
one Almighty. 


So the Father is God, the Son is ; Ged, 
and the Holy Crhoſt is God; and yet they 


are not three Gods, but one God. 


So likewiſe the Father is Lord, the Son is 


Lord, and the . Ghoſt is Lord; and yet 
they are not three Lords, but one Lord. © 
For as we are compelled by the chriſtian 


truth, to acknowledge every perſon by him- 
foo be Gad end Loed. 4 


So are we forbidden by the catholic reli- 
gion to fay there are three Gods, or three 
Lords. 

The Father is made of no one, neither 
created, nor begotten. 

The Son is from the Father alone not 
made, nor created, but begotten, 

The Holy Ghoſt is from the Father and 


the Son; 4 made, nor created, nor be- 


gotten. but proceeding. 
So there is one Father, not three Fathers; 


one Son, not three Sons; and one Holy 
Ghoſt, not three Holy Ghoſts. 

And in this Trinity there is nothing before 
or after, nothing greater or lefſ:r ; but the 
whole three perſons are co-eternal to one 
another, and co-equal._ 

So that in all bins, = hos dd ate 
ſaid above, the Unity is to be worſhipped in 
Trinity, and the Trinity in Unity. *

Of your recreations. 

Let your intention be pure in your recre- 
ations : as far as they are flary for the 
heaith of your body, or the relaxation of your 
mind, they are agreeable to the will of God, 
and no farther ; and this will of God ought 
to be your rule, both in die choice of them, 
and in the meaſure of time you allow for 
them. 12 | 

Fly then all ſuch recreations as are finful 
or dangerous; all ſuch as tend to ſoften the 
toul, and fill it with the ſpirit and love of 
this world; all ſuch as favour of the pomps of 
Satan, which you have renounced ; all ſuch, 
in fine, as inſtead of being really ſerviceable 


tor the relaxation of the mind, or the health 
oi the body, are prejudicial either to the one 


or to the other, or to both. | 
Allow no more time for your recreations 
than is neceſſary for thoſe ends for which re-

uſe of their precious time !

glory of God, fo you ought alſo frequently 
in the midſt of them to recollect yourſelf in

An OBLATION after COMMUNION. 


O Father of mercies, and God of all con- 

ſolation, how haſt thou loved us, to 
whom thou haſt given thy only begotten Son 
once for our ranſom, and daily for the food 
of our ſouls! What can I, a wretched crea-

Verily nothing elſe but this fame beloved 
Son of thine, whom thou haſt given to me,

greater, or more worthy of thyſelf, Him 
then 1 offer to thee, O heavenly Father, with

CoNTIRMATION. 


I. Un firmation is a ſacrament, by which the 
faithful, who have already been made 
children of God by their baptiſm, receive the 
Holy Ghoſt by the prayer and impoſition of 
the hands of the biſhops, the ſucceſſors of the 
apoſtles (Acts viii. 14, Sc. xix. 5, 6.) in or- 
der to their being made, ſtrong and perfect 


Chriſtians, and valiant ſoldiers of Feſus Chriſt. 


It is called confirmation from its effect, which 
is to confirm or firengthen thoſe that receive it, 
in the profeſſion of the true faith; to give 
them ſuch courage and reſolution as to be 
willing rather to die than to turn from it; and 
to arm them in general againſt all their ſpi- 
ritual enemies. 

2. This ſacrament was originally deſigned, 
and inſtituted by our Lord, for all Chriſtians; 
and conſequently is a d:vine ordinance, which 
all are obliged to comply with; and is fo ne- 
cellary for all, that the neglect of receiving it 

would 


tal fin

3. The principal effects of this ſacrament 
are, a fort:fj in: grace, in order to ſtrengthen 
the ſoul againſt all the viſible and inviſible 
enemies of the faith; and a certain dedica- 
tion and conſecration of the ſoul by the Holy 
Ghoſt, the mark of which dedication and 
conſecration is left in the ſoul, as a character 
which can never be effaced. 

4. Hence this ſacrament can be received 


but once; and it would be a ſacrilege to at- 


tempt to recei e it a ſecond time: for which 
rcaſon alſo, the faithful are bound to take an 
extreme care to come to this ſacrament dul 


diſpoſed ; left, if they ſhould be ſo unhappy

riſk of being deprived for ever of the grace

HE five joyful myſteries. 1, The an- 
nunciation of the angel Gabriel, and the 
incarnation of the Son of God in the womb 
of the Bleſſed Virgin. 2. The viſitation of 
St. Elizabeth. 3. The nativity or birth of 
our Lord. 4. The preſentation of our Lord 
in the temple, and the purification of his 
Bleſſed Mother. 5. His being found in the 
temple in the midſt of the doctors, after 


3 been loſt three days, by his Mother 


and her chaſte ſpouſe St. Joſeph. 


The five delorous or ſorrowful myſteries, 
1. The prayer of our Lord in the garden, 
with his agony and ſweat of blood. 2. His 
being ſcourged at the pillar. 3. His being 
crowned with thorns, and abuſed by the ſol- 
diers. 4. His carriage of the croſs. 5. His 
crucifixion and death. 


The five glorious myſteries. I. The re- 
ſurrection of our Lord. 2. His aſcenſion 
into heaven. 3. The coming down of the 
Holy Ghoſt. 4. The aſſumption of the 
Bleſſed Virgin. 5. Her eternal felicity, and 
that of all the bleſſed in the kingdom of 


Heaven.

to breed up ſouls, and fit them for the other; 
and how are they fitted, but by an eager de- 
fire of enjoying God, their only end ? 
Break my froward ſpirit, O Jeſus, make 


it humble and obedient : grant me grace to 


depart hence with contempt of this world, 


and a heart filled with joy at my going to 


_ thee. 


Let the memory of thy paſſion make me 
ehearfully undergo all temptations or ſuffer- 
ings here for thy love, whilit my ſoul breathes 
after that bliſsful life, and immortal glory, 
which thou haſt prepared in heaven for thy 
ſervants. 5 

O Jeſus, let me frequently and attentively 


conſider, that whatever J gain, if I loſe thee, 


all is loſt: and whatever I loſe, if I gain thee, 


all is gained. 


Have merey, &c. as in the fifth Petition.
